The 2026 FIFA World Cup is the first 48-team World Cup in tournament history, co-hosted by the United States, Canada, and Mexico across 16 host cities, with 104 matches across 39 days from June 11 through the Final on July 19, 2026. This is the running ColorWay Sports tracker grading every match kit and uniform pairing across the entire tournament. Every match gets a grade, every kit matchup gets analyzed, every Adidas, Nike, Puma, and other-brand reveal gets tracked. Live updates from kickoff in Group A through the Final.

## What This Tracker Grades

We grade every match kit and uniform pairing for visual contrast, color cohesion, and broadcast read. Each match gets a letter or numeric grade based on how the home team's chosen kit and the away team's chosen kit pair against the pitch, the broadcast frame, and each team's brand identity. We track every home kit + away kit decision at the group stage and through every knockout round, and we flag the games where one team's choice elevates or downgrades the pairing.

For the standalone rankings of every individual home kit, away kit, and third kit released for the 2026 World Cup, see our content cluster of ranking posts at the bottom of this tracker. This tracker is the live MATCH-BY-MATCH grading layer that sits on top of those rankings as the tournament plays.

*Kit photography throughout this tracker courtesy of adidas, Nike, Puma, and the respective official kit suppliers and national federations.*

Switzerland against Algeria is another tidy red-versus-white knockout matchup, and a slightly plainer one than most. Switzerland wear the Puma home, the clean red shirt with white side panels and the white Swiss cross on the chest, against Algeria in their adidas white home with the green and red trim and the Fennecs crest. The contrast is perfectly clear and broadcast-friendly, but both kits keep things fairly minimal, so there is less pattern personality than the round's louder pairings. A clean, functional clash that does its job without much flourish. Six and a half out of 10.

Portugal against Croatia is a vibrant alternate-versus-alternate Round of 32 pairing that punches at the top of the round. Portugal wear their Puma green away, the teal-tinted ocean-wave chevron alternate that landed near the top of our away-kit rankings, against Croatia in the Nike blue away with the tonal royal-and-navy checkerboard pattern and the HNS crest. Green against blue is a cool-on-cool color story that could sit flat with two dull kits — but both sides bring so much pattern personality that the matchup pops on the broadcast: the Portuguese wave chevron sweeping across the front against the tonal Croatian checkerboard, two design-first alternates carrying every camera angle. Both federations reaching for their most distinctive shirts rather than the primaries lifts the ceiling, the tonal separation between the teal-leaning green and the royal blue keeps the read crisp, and the two crests both carry serious brand weight. Nine and a half out of 10.

Spain against Austria is a clean, classic red-versus-white knockout pairing. Spain wear the adidas home, the deep red shirt with navy sleeves, gold trim, and the crest over the heart, against Austria in their Puma white change kit with the soft tonal pattern and the black ÖFB eagle. Red against white is one of the most reliable, broadcast-friendly contrasts in the sport, and both crests read cleanly at distance. It lands a touch below the round's boldest clashes because Austria's understated white away keeps the pairing more tasteful than loud, but the separation is sharp and the identities are clear. Seven out of 10.

The USMNT wear the Nike home, the white shirt with the bold wavy red stripes, the navy collar, and the USA shield crest, against Bosnia in the Kelme royal blue home with the fine yellow pinstripes, blue-and-gold collar trim, and the national flag crest. Red-and-white stripes against saturated royal blue is a genuinely vibrant color story, both kits committing to a full national identity with real pattern personality. The wavy USA red pops off the Bosnia blue at every camera angle and Bosnia's yellow pinstripes add just enough warmth to keep the two sides from sitting flat. A confident, broadcast-friendly primary-versus-primary pairing that lands near the top of the round. Nine out of 10.

Belgium against Senegal turns into one of the most visually loud alternate-versus-alternate matchups of the round. Belgium pull the adidas blue-and-pink away, the Magritte-inspired surrealist alternate with the tropical pattern that landed near the top of our away-kit rankings, against Senegal in their Puma green away with the yellow and red trim and the Lions of Teranga crest. The Belgian blue and pink is one of the boldest change kits in the tournament and it reads sharp against Senegal's green without either side going dull, the surrealist pattern popping up close while the broad tonal separation carries the broadcast frame. Both teams passing on their primary shirts usually caps the ceiling, but the Belgian alternate carries so much personality on its own that the pairing keeps the punch. A confident, high-identity kit clash. Eight out of 10.

England against Congo DR is a clean white-versus-blue Round of 32 jersey and uniform pairing. England wear their Nike white home, the Three Lions shirt with the red and blue trim and the national crest, against Congo DR in their Umbro sky blue home with the tonal zebra-stripe pattern, the red collar and cuff trim, and the Leopards crest. White against blue is one of the most dependable contrasts in the sport, and Congo DR's sky blue is bright enough to pop off England's clean white without going dark-heavy on the broadcast. The zebra-stripe detailing on the Leopards' shirt adds real texture up close, and England's white stays crisp and legible as the neutral counterpart. It lands solidly in the same band as the round's other strong white-versus-color finals rather than the top tier — the sky blue is a lighter partner than a saturated navy or royal would be — but the matchup is tidy, high-identity, and broadcast-friendly. Seven and a half out of 10.

Ivory Coast wear their vivid Puma orange home, Les Éléphants in the saturated national kit with the green and white trim and the elephant crest, against Norway in the all-white alternate Nike kit with the red and blue trim and the NFF crest. The Ivorian orange is one of the more vibrant primary identities in the tournament and it pops hard against Norway's clean white, the white-on-orange contrast staying sharp under the lights without the dark-color heaviness that can crowd a broadcast frame. Norway swapping out of the primary red and into the white alternate keeps the pairing crisp instead of fighting the orange head-on, and the Scandinavian red and blue trim adds enough warmth to keep the white side from going plain. A confident, broadcast-friendly look. Eight and a half out of 10.

White against blue is one of the most legible contrasts in the sport, and Germany's crisp white reads instantly on the broadcast while Paraguay's deep blue anchors the dark side of the frame. The Paraguay print adds real movement up close, though the tonal swirl softens a touch at distance, and Germany's white stays as iconic a uniform as the tournament has. Yellow against navy is a clean, high-energy contrast that separates beautifully under the lights, Ecuador's brightness popping hard against Germany's deep blue. It lands a half-step shy of a perfect 10 only because navy reads a touch darker than the bold royal blue that powers the very best blue-on-warm clashes — but this is a genuinely great-looking matchup. Nine and a half out of 10.

Brazil's yellow is the most recognizable kit on the planet, and Scotland's navy is a rich, classy foil that only makes it pop harder. This is exactly the kind of vivid, complementary color-on-color matchup that earns the top mark. Ten out of 10.

The šahovnica is unmistakable, a bold graphic that needs no help standing out, and Panama's clean navy is exactly the right foil for it — a calm, classy dark base that lets Croatia's checks do the talking without ever fading into them. Navy against red-and-white is a strong, high-contrast pairing, and the combination of an iconic pattern on one side and a handsome, underrated third kit on the other makes this a genuinely great-looking matchup. It's a half-step under the elite color-on-color clashes only because navy is the quieter partner, but it's still one of the better pairings of the group stage. Nine out of 10.

White against gold is a warmer, more unusual pairing than the usual light-versus-dark, and it works beautifully: England's shirt is all clean classic restraint and Ghana's is bursting with color and culture, so the two play off each other without ever blurring together. Ghana's gold is one of the most distinctive kits in the entire tournament and England's white is timeless; on the broadcast it reads vivid, high-contrast, and full of personality. It falls a half-step short of a perfect 10 only because white-and-gold doesn't quite detonate the way the great complementary clashes do. Nine out of 10.

Portugal brought out their deep red Puma home — a rich garnet shirt with a green collar and cuff trim and the Portugal crest on the chest — against Uzbekistan's white away, a clean sheet with royal blue piping, a subtle tonal diamond ikat pattern, and a green-and-blue collar. Red against white is the most dependable contrast in the sport, and both shirts are sharp and well-tailored, so it reads perfectly clean on the broadcast. The catch is that it just doesn't pop the way Portugal's red did against Congo DR's blue back in the opener — Uzbekistan's white is tidy but quiet, and the ikat detailing mostly washes out from distance. A solid, no-drama light-versus-dark matchup that does its job without turning any heads. Seven out of 10.

Norway came out in their stealth blackout kit — an all-black Nike shirt with a tonal flag-cross crest and a blacked-out swoosh, moody and modern and quietly one of the best change kits in the tournament. Senegal answered in their gorgeous white Puma home, a pale shirt loaded with a soft pastel African pattern and a sharp green collar, one of the most culturally rich designs of the whole World Cup. Black against white is the cleanest contrast in football, and with two genuinely strong, distinctive shirts on either end it looks fantastic on camera. Norway's kits are a personal favorite around here and even the black version carries that, while Senegal's white is close to a work of art. Nine out of 10.

France against Iraq is a clean, classic blue-versus-white that does its job without ever quite popping. France brought the deep navy-blue Nike home — that rich tonal-patterned shirt with the white polo collar and the gold rooster crest, one of the best-looking home kits in the entire tournament. Iraq countered in their white away, a tidy shirt carrying the tonal green Lions of Mesopotamia graphic. Dark blue against white always separates cleanly on the broadcast, and France's blue is strong enough to carry the pairing on its own, but blue-versus-white is about as standard as a kit matchup gets — there is no bold complementary clash here to push it into the upper tier. A sharp, broadcast-friendly look anchored by a great French home shirt. Seven and a half out of 10.

</div>

Argentina against Austria is one of the best-looking pairings of the group stage so far, a genuine showcase of two strong national identities. Argentina wore the timeless Albiceleste — those unmistakable sky-blue-and-white vertical stripes with the gold-trimmed AFA crest, maybe the most iconic home shirt in the entire tournament. Austria answered in a sharp, saturated red Puma home with black sleeve panels, white cuff striping, and the eagle crest, a bold solid that pushes hard against Argentina's pale stripes. Light celeste-and-white against deep red is a vivid, near-complementary clash that separates instantly at every distance — on the broadcast, on a phone, from the back row — with no muddiness anywhere. The only thing keeping it a half-step under a perfect 10 is that the stripes-versus-solid contrast, gorgeous as it is, doesn't quite detonate the way the tournament's very best orange-on-blue clashes do. A beautiful, high-class matchup and one of the best of the round. Nine and a half out of 10.

New Zealand against Egypt is a clean white-versus-red pairing that reads fine but never rises above functional. New Zealand wore their white Puma change kit, a pale cloud-and-fern patterned shirt that is tasteful but quiet — it reads almost grey-white from distance and lacks the punch of a true bright white. Egypt brought their red Puma home with its tonal geometric print, the stronger of the two shirts and the one doing most of the work here. White against red should separate cleanly, and it does, but neither kit brings much saturation or personality to the screen — the Kiwi white is muted and the contrast stays polite rather than vivid. A tidy, watchable matchup with little to remember it by. Six out of 10.

Uruguay against Cape Verde is the tournament's sixth perfect 10, and it is a gorgeous one. Uruguay came in the iconic Celeste — that unmistakable sky-blue La Celeste home with the white collar and navy trim, one of the most distinctive base colors in world football. Cape Verde answered in a bold red, their brand-new Capelli third kit: a deep red with navy side panels and a tonal geometric print, worn by a debutant nation that looks like it belongs on the biggest stage. Sky blue against red is a vivid, near-complementary clash that detonates at every distance — on the broadcast, from the back row, on a phone screen — the light Celeste and the saturated red separating instantly with zero muddiness. Both shirts are beautiful on their own and electric together, and the Cape Verde red is one of the best change kits of the entire tournament. There is nothing to dock. Ten out of 10, and one of the best-looking matchups we have graded all tournament.

Belgium against Iran is a clean red-versus-white matchup carried by one of the boldest reds in the tournament. Belgium came in their Red Devils adidas home, that deep, saturated red with black-and-gold detailing — a powerful, instantly recognizable shirt that anchors the whole pairing. Iran answered in their white Majid home, a sharp, modern kit with tricolor flag trim and a subtle tonal cheetah-print across the sleeves. Red against white is a textbook high-contrast pairing that separates cleanly from every seat and reads sharply on the broadcast, with Belgium's vivid red doing the heavy lifting. It earns a 7.5 on the strength of that Belgian red — a step above a standard light-versus-dark because one side brings a genuinely excellent shirt. Seven and a half out of 10.

Spain against Saudi Arabia is a clean, classic red-versus-white pairing that does its job without ever quite catching fire. La Roja came in their deep adidas red home, the navy-and-gold shoulder trim and the crowned crest giving it real heritage weight, a strong and saturated shirt. Saudi Arabia answered in their white adidas change kit, a sharp, understated look with gold-and-green collar detailing and the falcon crest. Red against white is one of the most reliable contrasts in the sport: it separates cleanly from every seat and reads instantly on the broadcast, with Spain's bold red doing most of the heavy lifting. It lands at a solid 7 because, like every white-versus-color matchup, it leans on one team to bring all the color rather than detonating from both ends — tidy and broadcast-friendly, just without the pop of the tournament's best. Seven out of 10.

Japan against Tunisia is a crisp blue-versus-white matchup carried by one of the tournament's sharpest home shirts. Japan came in their Samurai Blue adidas home, that deep, clean royal blue that has become one of the most recognizable kits in Asian football — simple, saturated, and beautifully broadcast-friendly. Tunisia answered in their white Kappa change kit, a tidy look with red trim, the eagle-feather shoulder print, and the Eagles of Carthage crest. Blue against white is a textbook high-contrast pairing that separates cleanly from every seat, and Japan's rich blue gives it more punch than most white-versus-color games manage. It edges up to a 7.5 on the strength of that Japan blue — a notch above a standard light-versus-dark because one side brings a genuinely excellent shirt. Seven and a half out of 10.

Ecuador against Curaçao is a sun-and-sea color clash that pops off the screen. La Tri came in their first-choice yellow, the bright Marathon home shirt with navy collar and cuffs, red trim, and the FEF crest on the chest — one of the most vivid base colors any nation brings to the tournament. Curaçao answered in the royal-blue adidas home that turned heads against Germany, deep cobalt carrying the yellow-and-cyan Korsou flag accents on the shoulder, a tiny Caribbean nation looking every bit like it belongs. Bright yellow against royal blue is one of the great high-contrast pairings in the sport: the two shirts separate instantly and read vividly from the back row and on the broadcast, and Curaçao's flag-yellow detailing quietly echoes Ecuador's primary so the matchup feels almost designed. It lands just shy of the perfect 10s only because yellow-against-blue, for all its punch, never quite detonates the way the pure orange-against-blue complementary clashes do — but this is top-tier stuff, saturated and joyful from both ends of the pitch. Nine and a half out of 10.

Germany against Ivory Coast is a clean white-versus-orange pairing carried by one of the best change-of-pace colors in the tournament. The Germans came in their minimalist white adidas home — crisp and classic, the DFB crest and understated trim doing all the work, exactly the restrained look they have worn for generations. Ivory Coast answered in the vibrant orange Puma home, a genuine standout: a tonal leopard-print body, green flank panels, and the elephant crest with its three stars, one of the most distinctive and joyful kits in the field. White against that orange separates beautifully on the broadcast and reads from any seat — Germany's blank canvas lets the Ivorian orange pop even harder. It lands just under the tournament's saturated best because, like every white-versus-color pairing, it leans on one team to bring all the color rather than detonating from both sides. Still, a sharp, broadcast-friendly matchup lifted by a gorgeous orange. Seven and a half out of 10.

Netherlands against Sweden is a complementary-color knockout and the tournament's newest perfect 10. Orange versus blue sit directly opposite each other on the color wheel — the single most powerful contrast in the sport — and both teams brought fully saturated, beautifully made versions of it. The Dutch came in the iconic Oranje home, that unmistakable burnt-orange Nike shirt with the black lion crest and tonal trim, one of the most recognizable kits on the planet. Sweden answered not in their usual yellow but in the blue adidas away, and it might be the best change shirt of the entire tournament: a deep royal blue laced with tonal gold filigree scrollwork, gold trim, and the Sverige crest, a retro-modern stunner. Orange against that blue detonates at every distance — on the broadcast, from the back row, on a phone screen — with zero muddiness and no clash to engineer around. Both shirts are gorgeous on their own and electric together. There is nothing to dock. Ten out of 10, and one of the best-looking matchups we have graded all tournament.

Türkiye against Paraguay is a tidy blue-versus-white pairing that never gets either team into its best clothes. Both nations live in red — Türkiye's iconic scarlet home and Paraguay's red-and-white stripes — so the colors clashed and pushed both sides to their change kits, and the matchup pays for it. Türkiye wore the white Nike away, a crisp shirt cut across the chest by a red marbled band carrying the crescent-and-star flag, clean but the muted half of their wardrobe. Paraguay answered in the navy Puma away, a moody storm-print shirt in dark blue and teal with the APF crest — a genuinely handsome change strip, yet still not the candy-stripe identity that makes the Albirroja instantly recognizable. White against navy-blue reads cleanly enough on the broadcast and the contrast holds from distance, but with both teams off their signature looks there is no spark, no heritage hook, no saturated collision to lift it. The what-if is loud: Türkiye's red against Paraguay's stripes would have been a far richer picture. As played, it is clean, competent, and a touch forgettable. Six and a half out of 10.

Brazil's blue away against Haiti's white home is the rare change-kit pairing that actually gains from the swap. Brazil's first choice is the amarelinha, but pushed into their Jordan away they brought one of the best alternate shirts in the field: a deep royal blue laced with a tonal diagonal pinstripe, gold trim, and flashes of green at the hem — saturated, modern, and unmistakably Brazil even without the yellow. Haiti, back on the World Cup stage for the first time since 1974, wore the white SAETA home with the red collar and the subtle grey heritage scene along the hem, understated and rich with story. Blue against white is a clean, high-contrast call that separates beautifully on the broadcast, and Brazil's blue carries far more punch than the plain change strips that left earlier light-versus-color pairings stuck at clean-and-classic. What holds it just shy of the top tier is the quiet half: Haiti's white is handsome but muted, and the matchup never reaches the four-color detonation of the tournament's best. Still, a vibrant, broadcast-friendly pairing lifted by a genuinely gorgeous Brazil away. Seven and a half out of 10.

Scotland against Morocco is a clean blue-versus-white pairing that leaves real points on the table. The Tartan Army wore the navy home — a deep, handsome adidas shirt with a tonal diamond weave and the Lion Rampant crest, one of the more underrated home kits in the field. Morocco came out in the white Puma away, a crisp shirt with a tonal zellige-tile print and a red-and-green collar nodding to the flag — clean, but the muted half of their wardrobe. Navy against white separates cleanly on the broadcast and is easy to read, yet it never ignites; both shirts lean on tonal prints that wash to plain at distance. The what-if writes itself: had Morocco worn their iconic red, this would have been a rich, saturated red-on-navy collision worth several points more. As played, it is tidy but unremarkable. Six out of 10.

United States against Australia was the most color-saturated pairing the tournament has served up, and both teams swung for the fences in their first-choice homes. The Americans broke out the showpiece — a white Nike home shirt ripped through with bold wavy red stripes, part flag and part heat-map, anchored by a navy collar and the USA shield, easily the busiest and most patriotic kit in the field. Australia answered in the Socceroos' unmistakable gold, green trim and the kangaroo-and-emu crest grounding one of the most distinctive home looks in the World Cup. Red-and-white stripes against solid gold is a riot of warm color that has every right to clash, yet the white in the American shirt and the saturated Aussie gold each hold their own lane, so it reads loud but never muddy on the broadcast. It comes up just shy of perfect only because the stripe pattern and the gold both fight for the eye rather than playing clean complements. Nine out of 10 — two confident, colorful homes and the boldest matchday look of the group stage.

Mexico against South Korea was the moodiest pairing of the matchday, a dark-on-dark color story that still found a way to separate. The hosts skipped the green home for a blacked-out adidas change kit — black with a tonal Aztec print, green collar and cuffs, and the green-and-red eagle crest — a clean, modern look that deliberately let South Korea's shirt do the talking, and talk it did. The Taegeuk Warriors' purple Nike away is one of the boldest kits in the entire field: a swirled, marbled wash of violet finished with a neon-edged tiger crest, and it reads like nothing else in the tournament. Black against that purple is unconventional and genuinely stylish, the kind of primetime pairing that pops on the broadcast, though with both shirts living in deep tones it stops just short of the clean high-value separation the tournament's top matchups get. The purple alone is nearly worth the price of admission. Eight out of 10.

Canada against Qatar is a clean light-versus-dark pairing born from a clash both teams had to dodge. Canada's red home and Qatar's maroon home are close enough in tone that one had to give, so both went to change kits — and the result is one of the more striking looks of the matchday. Canada broke out their black Nike alternate, a moody shirt splashed with a white cracked-ice graphic, the red maple-leaf crest and red swoosh popping against the dark base. Qatar answered in their clean white adidas away, the maroon shoulder stripes and the QFA crest the only color on an otherwise minimal shirt. Black against white is maximum-value contrast and separates instantly on the broadcast, Canada's busy graphic carrying the visual interest while Qatar keeps it understated. It is sharp and broadcast-friendly without a true color-on-color pop, landing right in the clean-but-classic tier. Seven out of 10.

Switzerland against Bosnia and Herzegovina is a sharp, classic red-versus-white pairing lifted by two genuinely strong shirts. Switzerland are back in their red after going green on the road against Qatar, and the Puma home is one of the better reds at the tournament: a clean, saturated scarlet with subtle white side panels, the Swiss cross, and the SFV crest. Bosnia counter in the away white that remains one of the tournament's quiet sleepers, the Kelme design out of Sarajevo with blue raglan sleeves and thin blue and yellow pinstripes. Red against white is one of the oldest clean contrasts in the sport and it separates perfectly on the broadcast, the Swiss red reading bold and the Bosnian white crisp beside it. It edges just ahead of the tournament's other red-versus-white pairings on the strength of Switzerland's deep home red, even if the pinstripe detailing on the Bosnia shirt washes out from distance. Seven and a half out of 10.

Czechia against South Africa is a warm, high-energy clash of two first-choice home kits, and the first time we have seen Czechia in their actual home shirt this tournament. With no red-on-red conflict this time — South Africa's gold sits nowhere near it — Czechia finally broke out the red Puma home, a rich crimson with crisp white trim and the gold double-tailed lion crest. South Africa countered in the canary gold adidas home that pays tribute to the 2010 Bafana Bafana shirt, green framing the collar and cuffs. Red against gold is a vivid, sun-warm color-on-color pairing in the same family as Ghana's gold against Panama's red, two saturated kits with real national identity that read instantly under the lights. The only thing keeping it a half-step below that one is South Africa's green trim, which blurs the separation just a touch in the wide shots. Eight out of 10.

Uzbekistan against Colombia is a clean light-versus-dark pairing with both sides in their change kits. Uzbekistan, making their first-ever World Cup appearance, came out in their white away shirt, royal blue piping tracing the raglan seams, a tonal ikat-style diamond pattern down the front, and the UFA crest at the chest. Colombia answered in their teal-and-navy adidas change kit, a dense tonal chevron texture in navy over teal with bright yellow trim that reads as a deep blue under the broadcast lights rather than the canary yellow most fans associate with Los Cafeteros. White against that dark blue-green separates cleanly from every camera angle, but neither side brought its boldest look — Uzbekistan's white reads a touch plain at distance and Colombia's pattern muddies into a near-solid dark mass on screen. A tidy, broadcast-friendly contrast without a real color-on-color pop. Seven out of 10.

England against Croatia is the matchup that proves a 10 does not have to be color-on-color. England bring the classic: a crisp white Nike home with navy and red trim at the collar and cuffs, red side flashes, the Three Lions crest beneath a lone gold star, and "Happy and Glorious" stitched inside the neck. Croatia counter in their blue change kit, a gorgeous tonal checkerboard in royal and navy that nods to the famous red-and-white Croatian checkerboard without ever clashing with England's white. Pure white against rich blue is one of the oldest and cleanest contrasts in the sport, and here it reads immaculately, both kits crisp and unmissable from every camera angle with no muddiness anywhere on the pitch. We usually save the top of the board for the boldest color-on-color pairings, but this one earns its perfect score on sheer class. Ten out of 10.

Portugal against Congo DR is exactly the kind of bold color-on-color pairing that lights up the board. Portugal bring their deep garnet-red Puma home, the green collar and cuff trim nodding to the flag and the Portugal crest sitting proud on the chest. Congo DR answer in a stunning sky-blue Umbro shirt, a tonal zebra-stripe pattern rippling across it with red collar and cuff trim and the Leopards crest. Red against bright blue is a textbook complementary clash, the two kits separating cleanly and reading vividly from every seat in the stadium and every frame of the broadcast, and the extra detailing, Portugal's tonal wave graphic and Congo DR's animal-print texture, gives the pairing real depth beyond the color pop. It is one of the most vibrant matchups of the tournament so far, sitting right in the top tier just shy of the perfect 10s. Nine and a half out of 10.

Austria against Jordan is the rare pairing where two first-choice home kits work against each other rather than with each other. Austria's home is a sharp, saturated red Puma shirt with black sleeve panels, white cuff striping, and the eagle crest. Jordan's real home, the Kelme kit, is mostly white, but it loads its shoulders and sleeves with a graduated red dot pattern and red trim, so the loudest color it brings to the pitch is the same red Austria is already wearing. Instead of a bold complementary clash, the orange-vs-blue and green-vs-sky-blue pairings that top this board, you get two kits echoing the same red with Jordan's white base sitting quietly behind it. It reads cleanly enough on the broadcast, but the tones are too similar and there is no true color-on-color contrast to make it pop. It lands as the lowest grade on the board so far. Six out of 10.

Argentina bring the most iconic stripes in the sport, the sky-blue-and-white of the Albiceleste, against Algeria's green change kit, and the result is a rich, high-value pairing. The Argentina home is a timeless look, those vertical celeste-and-white bars instantly readable and carrying the gold-trimmed AFA crest. Algeria counter in a clean green, the Fennec Foxes color popping hard against the pale Argentine stripes for maximum color separation. Green against sky-blue-and-white is a vivid complementary contrast that reads beautifully on the broadcast, two strong national identities with nothing washing out. It sits comfortably in the upper tier without quite reaching the bold complementary clashes at the very top of the board. Eight out of 10.

This one is carried, top to bottom, by Norway. Their red Nike home, the bold navy-and-white flag cross slashing across a deep red shirt, is simply the best kit at the entire 2026 World Cup, and for us it is not particularly close. Put Norway on the pitch and the matchup starts at a 9.5 before the other team even shows up. Iraq, in their white change kit with the tonal green lion graphic, is the part we are not sold on, the busy lion doing little for us and the kind of shirt that would drag most pairings down a notch. Here it does not matter. Norway's red is so good that even a kit we are not fans of on the other side cannot pull this below a 9.5. White against red is clean, high-contrast, and broadcast-perfect, and the Norwegian cross does all the heavy lifting. Nine and a half out of 10, and just about every point of it belongs to Norway.

France's deep blue home against Senegal's white is the cleanest classic contrast on the board so far. France ran the Nike home, a rich midnight blue carrying a tonal diagonal slash pattern, a crisp white polo collar, and the gold coq crest, one of the most refined first-choice shirts at the tournament. Senegal answered in the Puma white home, a bright shirt with a soft pastel tonal print, a green collar, and the Lions of Teranga crest, the green trim giving the white just enough lift to keep it from reading plain. Blue against white is a timeless high-value pairing that separates instantly and reads perfectly on the broadcast, France's pattern adding depth on one side while Senegal's green-trimmed white stays clean on the other. It sits just below the bold complementary clashes at the top of the board, a sharp, classic, broadcast-friendly look. Eight out of 10.

Iran's white home against New Zealand's black change kit is a clean light-versus-dark pairing that reads well but lands at a modest 6. It is the All Whites' awkward fixture: New Zealand's own home is white too, so with Iran in their white Majid home — the tricolor flag trim at the collar and a subtle grey cheetah-print climbing the sleeves — the Kiwis were pushed all the way to their black change kit, a tonal Puma design with a koru-and-fern pattern and the silver fern on the chest. White against black is maximum value contrast and separates cleanly under the SoFi Stadium lights in Los Angeles, but neither shirt does much beyond the basics: Iran's white reads plain at distance, and New Zealand's black flattens to a near-solid dark mass on the broadcast with the fern pattern washing out. High-contrast and tidy, but low on color and personality, the quietest kind of clean. Six out of 10.

This is the color story of the matchday. Saudi Arabia's deep-green home — an Adidas kit loaded with a blue-and-purple geometric Sadu-weave pattern, one of the sharpest individual shirts of the entire tournament — against Uruguay's Celeste, the sky blue with the white polo collar and navy trim that is one of football's most storied identities. Green against sky blue is a fresh, high-contrast pairing you almost never get, and both teams showed up in their first-choice home kits with nothing to engineer around: deep saturated green on one side, soft luminous celeste on the other, both reading instantly and cleanly under the Hard Rock Stadium lights in Miami. It is the rare matchup where both shirts are individually top-tier and the pairing pops as a unit. The only thing keeping it off a 10 is that the contrast, excellent as it is, sits a hair below the complementary-clash ceiling of the orange-versus-blue tens. Nine out of 10 — the best-looking pairing since the perfect scores.

The Red Devils brought their iconic red — the most recognizable thing Belgium owns — and Egypt, whose own home shirt is also red, was pushed to the change strip: a crisp white Puma kit with a tonal pyramid print and a sharp black collar, a quiet sleeper of the African contingent. Red against white separates cleanly on the Lumen Field grass in Seattle and reads from any seat, but like the tournament's other red-versus-white matchups — Haiti–Scotland, Canada–Bosnia, Korea–Czechia, all sevens — it tops out at clean-and-classic rather than detonating, and the Egyptian print mostly washes to plain white at broadcast distance. Seven out of 10.

Spain's red home against Cape Verde's white change kit is the plainest of the tournament's red-versus-white pairings, and it lands a half-step under the cluster of 7s for it. The story is all Cape Verde: the Blue Sharks (Tubarões Azuis) are at a World Cup for the first time in their history, the smallest nation by population in the field, and the moment is enormous. But the kit on the pitch is the muted half of their wardrobe. Their identity is the royal-blue home, and pushed off it by Spain's red they went to the white Capelli change shirt — clean, but the tonal geometric print and the thin blue-and-orange collar trim are the only things keeping it off a blank tee, and both vanish at broadcast distance. Spain answers in the classic La Roja red, iconic but rendered here as a fairly flat solid with navy accents and none of the gold-trim flourish of their best home shirts. Red versus white reads cleanly and always will, but this is the most basic version of it we have graded — no sleeper change kit like Bosnia's or Scotland's, no heritage hook like Haiti's. Six and a half out of 10. The history is a 10; the color story is the quietest of the tournament so far.

Sweden's yellow home against Tunisia's red home is the best warm-on-warm pairing the tournament has produced, and it clears the bar Australia-Türkiye set. Both teams came out in their first-choice home kits — no clash, no change shirt — and yellow against red is a high-energy, fully saturated collision that still separates cleanly because Sweden's Blågult yellow is a true bright primary, not a gold, so it reads a clear lane apart from the Tunisian red. The Carthage Eagles' Kappa red is sharp in its own right, the white eagle-feather brushstroke print climbing the shoulders and a crisp white collar lifting it above a plain solid. This is the same warm-on-warm category as Australia's gold versus Türkiye's red, but the brighter yellow gives it more separation and more pop, so it grades a notch higher. It stops just short of the complementary 10s because yellow and red still sit on the same warm half of the wheel rather than true opposites. Eight and a half out of 10 — two first-choice homes, maximum saturation, and the cleanest yellow-red read of the tournament.

The tournament's third perfect 10, and it is the second time the 2026 World Cup has served the platonic complementary clash: orange against blue. Ivory Coast's Les Éléphants in the Puma orange home is one of the boldest single-color identities in world football — a deep, burnt orange carrying a tonal leopard print that rewards every close-up — and Ecuador answers in a gorgeous midnight-navy change kit, the Marathon shirt finished with copper crest and trim. Here is the detail that makes it sing: Ecuador's first-choice home is yellow, and yellow against Ivory Coast's orange would have been a muddy adjacent-tone wash, one of the worst pairings the draw could produce. Pushed to their change shirt, La Tri went to the navy — and turned a near-clash into a complementary knockout. Orange versus blue is the cleanest pairing in the sport, the exact axis that earned the Netherlands and Japan a 10, and this one matches it: the burnt orange and the leopard texture against the deep navy and the copper detailing separate at every distance and reward every replay. There is nothing to dock. Ten out of 10 — the third perfect score of the tournament, and proof that a forced change kit, chosen well, can be the best decision on the pitch.

The second perfect 10 of the tournament, and it is pure pop art. The Netherlands in the Oranje home against Japan in the Samurai Blue home is orange versus blue, the single cleanest complementary pairing in football, both teams in their first-choice identity with nothing to engineer around. KNVB orange is the most recognizable one-color kit on earth and Japan's blue is its perfect foil — on the broadcast the two saturate opposite ends of the spectrum and separate from any seat and any camera angle. No clash, no compromise, no tonal print to wash out, just two iconic homes turning the pitch into a color-wheel demonstration. Ten out of 10, and one of the easiest we have handed out: orange against blue is the platonic complementary clash, and both federations showed up in their best.

The Germany whites are minimalist as ever, the DFB crest and black trim doing all the talking, and Curaçao's blue — accented with the yellow-and-cyan shoulder detail pulled straight from the Korsou flag — is one of the tournament's quiet sleepers, a tiny Caribbean nation's first World Cup kit reading sharp against the German white. White versus blue is a textbook contrast that holds at distance, and the David-and-Goliath story gives it a lift. It lands just short of the top tier because white-versus-color, however clean, never quite detonates the way a full complementary clash does. Seven and a half out of 10.

Haiti, back at a World Cup for the first time since 1974, wore the white SAETA shirt with a red collar and a subtle grey heritage scene along the hem — understated and rich with history. Scotland, pushed off their navy home by Haiti's light kit, pulled the retro red shirt with the navy pinstripes and lilac trim, an instant-classic adidas throwback that is arguably the better-looking of the two.   <p style="font-size: 1.5em; font-weight: 900; margin: 0; text-align: center; color: #9bc824;">Score: 10 / 10</p>
</div>

This is the one. Brazil against Morocco is the platonic ideal of a World Cup kit matchup, and it earns the tracker's first perfect score. Both teams came out in their first-choice home kits — no clash, no compromise, no change shirt forced by a color conflict — and the result is a full four-color spectacle: Brazil's canary yellow over blue shorts against Morocco's red over green. Yellow versus red is already a top-tier pairing, both fully saturated, both reading instantly from the back row and on every broadcast angle. Then add the secondaries — Brazil's blue, Morocco's green — and all four colors of two of the most beloved identities in the sport are on the grass at the same time. The amarelinha is the most famous shirt in football; Morocco's red-and-green is one of the boldest looks of the modern era; and together they do not fight, they complete each other. Earlier matchups topped out because a tonal print washed out at distance or a pairing never rose past red-versus-white. This one has none of those problems. There is nothing to dock: both teams in their best, a color story that detonates at every distance, zero conflict to engineer around. Ten out of 10. We would watch it on mute just for the kits.

Group B's second match is the boldest color clash of the tournament so far. Qatar in the home maroon against Switzerland's green Puma away is a true complementary pairing — deep garnet and mid-green sit on opposite sides of the color wheel — and it separates cleanly on the BC Place pitch instead of leaning on the light-versus-dark and red-versus-white contrasts that have defined most of the early grades. Switzerland's decision to go green on the road rather than the usual white is the more adventurous call of the two, a fresh change-kit identity for the Nati away from their red. The one thing keeping it from climbing higher is the accessibility math: red against green is the textbook color-blindness pairing, and for the roughly one in twelve male viewers with red-green deficiency the two sides lose some of their separation on screen. That, plus Qatar's maroon reading a shade dark at broadcast distance, is the gap between this and the USMNT's 8. Seven and a half out of 10 — the most adventurous pairing of the tournament so far, dinged only by who can and cannot fully see it.

The USMNT opened its first home World Cup since 1994 in the home white at SoFi, and the pairing delivered the best broadcast read of the tournament so far. White against Paraguay's dark navy Puma away — swirled with an abstract storm print that ranks among the boldest change kits in the field — is near-perfect contrast, and unlike the tonal prints that washed out at distance in the Azteca opener and in Korea-Czechia, the Paraguay storm graphic actually keeps its texture on camera. Paraguay's classic red-and-white striped home stayed in the bag precisely because the American white made the navy the right call, and there was no conflict in sight. Per Fanatics, USMNT gear is already the tournament's top seller, so the shirt that walked out at SoFi is the one most American closets are about to copy. Eight out of 10 — the cleanest, highest-contrast pairing we have graded, and the first one where both kits earned their close-ups.

The first men's World Cup match ever played in Canada, and the kits met the moment cleanly: Canada in the home red at BMO Field, Bosnia and Herzegovina in the away white, the cleanest pairing the fixture can produce. The Bosnia change shirt remains one of the tournament's quiet sleepers, a Kelme design from Sarajevo designer Haris Stambolic with a white base, blue raglan sleeves, and thin blue and yellow pinstripes, and the maple leaf red against it read exactly as advertised in Toronto. But like Korea against Czechia the night before, the pairing tops out at standard-issue red-versus-white, and the pinstripes that make the Bosnia shirt special on the rack are invisible from broadcast distance. Seven out of 10. Canadian soccer's biggest home afternoon ever looked clean, classic, and just a little safe.

Group A's second game handed FIFA the tournament's first kit conflict, and it resolved exactly as projected. South Korea's home shirt is red, Czechia's home shirt is red, and as the listed home team the Taegeuk Warriors kept the tiger-print red while Czechia came out in the away shirt for their first World Cup match since 2006: an icy crystal white loaded with a tonal print pulled from Bohemian cut glass, finished with gold on the collar and a gold double-tailed lion on the crest. Red against crystal white reads cleanly on the Estadio Akron grass and the gold Czech trim is a quiet luxury detail, but the pairing never rises past standard-issue red-versus-white, and Korea's tiger print mostly disappears at broadcast distance. Seven out of 10. Czechia's twenty-year World Cup return got a clean visual landing.

The 2026 World Cup opens with a kit pairing sixteen years in the making. Mexico against South Africa is the exact fixture that opened the 2010 World Cup at Soccer City in Johannesburg, on the exact same date, June 11, and now the kit story comes full circle with the host roles reversed. South Africa's canary gold home shirt is a direct tribute to the one Bafana Bafana wore that day in 2010, when Siphiwe Tshabalala hammered the tournament's opening goal past Mexico and gave the World Cup one of its most replayed goal celebrations. Mexico answers with the strongest home identity in CONCACAF, the deep verde loaded with a tonal Aztec sun stone print that reads nearly solid green from broadcast distance and rewards every close-up. Green against gold is a top-shelf color pairing in concept, and both teams showing up to a World Cup opener in their first-choice home looks is exactly how a tournament should start. But the broadcast told on this one: Mexico's tonal Aztec print reads as flat dark green from distance, South Africa's green trim blurs the separation in every wide shot, and a pairing that is a 10 on paper landed as merely good on screen. We are calling it a 7.5. The history is a 10; the contrast is not.


## Tournament Overview

- **Host countries:** United States, Canada, Mexico
- **Host cities:** 16 total (11 in USA, 2 in Canada, 3 in Mexico)
- **Total teams:** 48 (first-ever 48-team World Cup, expanded from the 32-team format)
- **Group stage:** 12 groups of 4 teams each, 72 matches
- **Knockout rounds:** Round of 32, Round of 16, Quarterfinals, Semifinals, Third Place, Final — 32 matches
- **Total matches:** 104
- **Tournament dates:** June 11, 2026 (Opener) through July 19, 2026 (Final)
- **Opener:** Mexico vs South Africa at Estadio Azteca, Mexico City (June 11, 3 PM ET)
- **Final:** July 19, 2026 at MetLife Stadium, East Rutherford, New Jersey

## Group Stage — All 12 Groups

The 2026 FIFA World Cup draw took place on December 5, 2025, at the Kennedy Center in Washington, D.C. All 12 groups are locked in below as the draw reference. Every match card lives in the chronological stream above.

### Group A — Mexico, South Africa, South Korea, Czechia

Group A is the host group anchored by Mexico, and it opened the entire tournament at Estadio Azteca on June 11 with both Matchday 1 games landing on opening day for US viewers: the opener at 3 PM ET in Mexico City, and South Korea against Czechia at 10 PM ET at Estadio Akron in Guadalajara.

### Group B — Canada, Bosnia and Herzegovina, Qatar, Switzerland

Group B is the second host group anchored by Canada, who open against Bosnia and Herzegovina in Toronto on June 12, the first men's World Cup match ever played on Canadian soil.

### Group C — Brazil, Morocco, Haiti, Scotland

Brazil headlines Group C alongside Morocco's 2022 semifinalist core and Scotland's return to the World Cup.

### Group D — United States, Paraguay, Australia, Türkiye

Group D is the third host group anchored by the United States Men's National Team, who open against Paraguay at SoFi Stadium on Friday June 12 at 9 PM ET on FOX, the biggest American soccer kit night since 1994.

### Group E — Germany, Curaçao, Ivory Coast, Ecuador

Germany leads Group E with three confederations represented.

### Group F — Netherlands, Japan, Sweden, Tunisia

Group F pairs the Dutch with the Samurai Blue, Sweden's classic yellow against the Tunisian red.

### Group G — Belgium, Egypt, Iran, New Zealand

Group G runs Belgium's Red Devils against three different continental kit traditions.

### Group H — Spain, Cape Verde, Saudi Arabia, Uruguay

Spain leads Group H with Uruguay providing the heritage matchup.

### Group I — France, Senegal, Norway, Iraq

Group I pairs the French with Senegal's 2002 quarterfinalist heritage and Norway's first World Cup appearance since 1998.

### Group J — Argentina, Algeria, Austria, Jordan

Defending champions Argentina headline Group J.

### Group K — Portugal, Congo DR, Uzbekistan, Colombia

Portugal leads Group K with Colombia providing the South American counterpoint.

### Group L — England, Croatia, Ghana, Panama

England closes the group draw with Croatia's classic red-and-white checkerboard kit and Ghana's iconic Black Stars yellow-and-red.

## Knockout Rounds

The expanded 48-team format introduces a Round of 32 for the first time in World Cup history. Knockout match cards join the stream above as the bracket fills.

- **Round of 32:** 16 matches (top 2 from each group + 8 best 3rd-place finishers across the 12 groups)
- **Round of 16:** 8 matches
- **Quarterfinals:** 4 matches
- **Semifinals:** 2 matches
- **Third Place:** 1 match
- **Final:** July 19, 2026 at MetLife Stadium

## Host Cities and Stadiums

### United States (11 host cities)

Arlington (AT&T Stadium), Atlanta (Mercedes-Benz Stadium), Boston (Gillette Stadium), East Rutherford NJ (MetLife Stadium — Final venue), Houston (NRG Stadium), Inglewood/LA (SoFi Stadium), Kansas City (Arrowhead Stadium), Miami (Hard Rock Stadium), Philadelphia (Lincoln Financial Field), San Francisco/Santa Clara (Levi's Stadium), Seattle (Lumen Field).

### Canada (2 host cities)

Toronto (BMO Field), Vancouver (BC Place).

### Mexico (3 host cities)

Guadalajara (Estadio Akron), Mexico City (Estadio Azteca — Tournament Opener venue), Monterrey (Estadio BBVA).

The 2026 FIFA World Cup is the biggest jersey and uniform tracker opportunity of the calendar year. 104 matches, 48 teams, three confederations of kit suppliers, and 39 days of group stage and knockout drama on the largest broadcast stage in sports. Every kit decision matters. Every contrast matters. Every match-by-match pairing tells a different visual story. ## Frequently Asked Questions

**What jersey is each team wearing in their World Cup match today?**

We log the exact home or away kit each team wears in every 2026 World Cup match the day it is played, so the fastest way to see what a team is wearing today is to find that match on the tracker above. Each match card shows both teams' kits, the color matchup, and our grade. We update it every day a match is played through the Final on July 19, so it stays current for whatever is on the schedule that day.

**When does the 2026 FIFA World Cup start and end?**

The 2026 FIFA World Cup runs from June 11, 2026 (Opener) through July 19, 2026 (Final). The Opener is Mexico vs South Africa at Estadio Azteca in Mexico City, and the Final is at MetLife Stadium in East Rutherford, New Jersey. The tournament runs 39 days end to end and covers 104 matches across 16 host cities in three host countries.

**Who is hosting the 2026 FIFA World Cup?**

The 2026 FIFA World Cup is co-hosted by the United States, Canada, and Mexico. The USA hosts 11 of the 16 host cities, Canada hosts 2, and Mexico hosts 3. The Final is in East Rutherford, New Jersey at MetLife Stadium and the Opener is in Mexico City at Estadio Azteca.

**How many teams are in the 2026 FIFA World Cup?**

The 2026 FIFA World Cup is the first 48-team World Cup in tournament history, expanded from the previous 32-team format. The 48 teams are split into 12 groups of 4 teams each for the group stage.

**How many matches are in the 2026 FIFA World Cup?**

The 2026 FIFA World Cup has 104 total matches: 72 group stage matches (12 groups × 6 matches per group) plus 32 knockout round matches (Round of 32, Round of 16, Quarterfinals, Semifinals, Third Place, and Final).

**What is the format of the 2026 FIFA World Cup knockout stage?**

The expanded 48-team format introduces a Round of 32 for the first time in World Cup history. The top 2 teams from each of the 12 groups plus the 8 best 3rd-place finishers across all 12 groups advance to the Round of 32. From there it is straight knockout through the Round of 16, Quarterfinals, Semifinals, Third Place playoff, and the Final.

**Which teams are in Group A of the 2026 FIFA World Cup?**

Group A of the 2026 FIFA World Cup features the host nation Mexico, South Africa, South Korea, and Czechia, who qualified through the UEFA play-offs in March 2026. Group A opened the tournament at Estadio Azteca in Mexico City on June 11, 2026, with Mexico vs South Africa.

**Which teams are in Group D with the United States?**

Group D features the United States, Paraguay, Australia, and Türkiye. The United States Men's National Team plays its group stage matches as the host of Group D across multiple US venues.

**Which group is the defending champion Argentina in?**

Argentina, the 2022 FIFA World Cup champions, are in Group J alongside Algeria, Austria, and Jordan.

**Which kit supplier is making the most 2026 World Cup jerseys?**

The 2026 FIFA World Cup kit landscape is dominated by Adidas, Nike, and Puma across the 48 qualified national teams. For our standalone rankings of every Adidas, Nike, and Puma World Cup 2026 kit, see our [Adidas home kit ranking](/stories/ranking-every-adidas-2026-world-cup-home-kit), [Adidas away kit ranking](/stories/ranking-every-adidas-2026-world-cup-away-kit), [Nike kit ranking](/stories/ranking-every-nike-world-cup-kit-2026), and [Puma kit ranking](/stories/ranking-every-puma-2026-world-cup-kit).

**How often is the 2026 FIFA World Cup jersey tracker updated?**

We update the tracker every day a match is played during the 2026 FIFA World Cup. Group stage matches get graded as they are played in real time, knockout round matches get added as the bracket fills, and the running averages and headline stat banners refresh through the full tournament.

**Where can I buy a 2026 FIFA World Cup jersey?**

Official national team jerseys from Adidas, Nike, Puma, and the other kit suppliers are available through the [national team jersey shop on Fanatics](https://fanatics.93n6tx.net/5kZn3j?u=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.fanatics.com%2Fsoccer-national-teams%2Fo-3690%2Bz-981768652-96597765%3Fvap%3D1). Every match card on this tracker also links directly to each team's gear.

**Where is the 2026 FIFA World Cup Final being played?**

The 2026 FIFA World Cup Final is being played on Sunday July 19, 2026 at MetLife Stadium in East Rutherford, New Jersey, just outside of New York City. MetLife Stadium is the home of the New York Giants and the New York Jets of the NFL and holds approximately 82,500 spectators for international soccer matches.
